MC	<p>130d_8e10</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CCAACCTAA --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>4869_ccc2</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TAAGCTTAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>3b73_9b0c</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CGAAGCGTT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>f8fb_2768</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>bd7e_bf33</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CCAGTGATA --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>0574_ae8b</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- AAGTATGTG --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>8fcd_ac61</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TAGGTTGGC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>02ef_5cfe</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TGCTGGCAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>392e_9e9f</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CCACATTCT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>c7bf_8618</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TTACTCCAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>d583_40de</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TCTTTCTAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse;
